## SPIRITS.

THE death of Marshal de Schomberg <note>Friedrich Hermann von Schomberg (1615–1690) was a world-renowned military commander and a Huguenot (French Protestant) who died at the Battle of the Boyne.</note> which afflicted all of England, was a subject of despair for me; and the fatal blow that took his life snatched away from me at the same time a Father, a Protector, and a Friend, whom I happily found united in his Person. I had laid the foundations of this great Bond <note>original: "Union"</note> in Brandenburg <note>A territory in the Holy Roman Empire (modern-day Germany) that became a haven for Protestant refugees.</note>, where entirely different motives had led us. Mr. de Schomberg had come there to seek the freedom of his Religion, which he

<summary>The author opens with a poignant tribute to the deceased Marshal de Schomberg, describing him as a surrogate father and protector. The narrative establishes their meeting in Brandenburg, driven by the Marshal's quest for religious liberty.</summary>

<keywords>Marshal de Schomberg, England, Brandenburg, religious freedom, Huguenots, friendship, mourning</keywords>

->THE ASSISTANT SPIRITS<-

could have found in France, nor in Portugal; and I had gone there to escape the fury of a Minister of State, an enemy of my Family, who had entirely ruined me in the King’s mind through false accusations, the least dark of which was that I had favored the stubbornness of the Huguenots <term>Huguenots: French Protestants who faced increasing persecution under Louis XIV, culminating in the revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</term> of Languedoc <term>Languedoc: A large province in Southern France; a historic center of Protestant resistance.</term>, instead of effectively employing the troops who were ordered to convert them <note>The "Dragonnades" were a policy under Louis XIV where soldiers were billeted in Huguenot homes to intimidate them into converting to Catholicism.</note>. I do not know if the quality of being a foreigner and an exile, which established some similarity in our destinies—or rather, his status as a victim of a Religion to which he had such a firm attachment, and which, though it was not my own, had nevertheless given rise to my misfortunes—inclined his heart toward wishing me well.

But it is true that he showed me all the consideration and all the tenderness imaginable. I ate only at his table, he included me in every gathering, and I accompanied him on all the journeys he made to Holland, without, however, ever having penetrated the mystery of his negotiations. It was impossible for me to resist the temptation of crossing the sea with him in the train of the Prince of Orange <note>William of Orange, who led the "Glorious Revolution" to claim the English throne in 1688.</note>, who was called

by the English, to deliver them from the budding despotism of his father-in-law <note>King James II of England, whose Catholic leanings and autocratic style led to his overthrow in the Glorious Revolution of 1688.</note>. Through his influence, I could have held great offices in that state; but as love for my homeland prevailed over ambition, I contented myself with being a spectator of a tragedy whose resolution surprised the whole world.

The Prince of Orange’s party having prevailed, and the entire nation having yielded—willingly or by force—under the power of his arms, he led them into Ireland to subdue rebel subjects, who were rightly termed "insurgents" <note>The Jacobite forces in Ireland who remained loyal to the deposed King James II.</note>. The good fortune that had accompanied him thus far did not see fit to abandon him in this expedition. It rendered him victorious over all these peoples. But Marshal de Schomberg <term>Marshal de Schomberg: Friedrich Hermann von Schomberg, a high-ranking military commander and Huguenot refugee who served as a general under William of Orange.</term>, who commanded his army, paid with his blood and his life for the victory of a battle fought with as much bravery as justice <note>The Battle of the Boyne (July 1, 1690), where Schomberg was killed while leading his troops across the river.</note>. -sels of an Irish Gentleman, who had the kindness to take me to his home. His house seemed made especially for an afflicted man; it was situated in the most remote part of this Kingdom, in a solitary place crisscrossed by ponds, woods, and meadows. Neither gold, nor silver, nor paintings, nor delicate architecture were to be seen there; but everything was convenient, clean, and well-arranged, and all the rooms did not fail to present, in their simplicity, a very pleasing sight to the eye. I was received with every possible welcome in this house; I had had the good fortune to render a considerable service to this gentleman, and at my request, and through the influence of the late Marshal <note>Marshal de Schomberg, the narrator's late patron.</note>, he had retained the property he owned in Ireland, which the Catholic administration <note>original: "la Catholicité"; referring to the government of King James II and the Jacobite forces during the Williamite War in Ireland, which had moved to confiscate the lands of those opposing them.</note> was about to confiscate. Gratitude led him to share in my affliction; he informed his family of the obligation he owed me and of the sorrowful state of my heart, so that upon my arrival, I found people already moved, who mingled their tears with mine. of what could console me. I was not yet capable of consolation. My pain seemed incurable to me, and all these offered remedies <note>In the 17th century, "remedies" often referred to philosophical arguments or spiritual advice intended to cure the "passions" of the soul, such as excessive grief.</note> served only to embitter it. I soon experienced that the strength of the human heart does not go so far as to endure a violent sadness for long, and that either we must reach our end, or the sadness must end. I had already spent two months shedding tears, fleeing from men, and seeking only dark places and grim objects, when my reason awoke as if from a deep sleep; and having perceived the disorder into which the excess of my grief had thrown all my faculties, the turmoil of my spirit, and the lethargy of my body, reason made me understand that our anxieties are useless to the dead, and that their rest is not established upon the ruin of that of the living, but only upon the fervor of our prayers, when they have deserved to reap the fruit thereof.

I appeared less withdrawn: the company of my host became more bearable to me, and we occasionally found ourselves together in his small library

—library <note>This text continues the word "library" from the previous page's final sentence.</note> which was supplied with very rare books. He knew many things and knew them as a gentleman <note>Original: "honnête homme." In the 17th and 18th centuries, this referred to a well-bred, cultured man who possessed social graces and a broad, non-specialized education.</note>. On my part, I ardently loved study and reading, and my experience with the people of the Court had sufficiently humanized all that I had learned in the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to traditional university education, which was often criticized at the time for being overly formal or "pedantic."</note>, when I was destined for the Church; so that our conversations had nothing pedantic about them, and we handled matters of Philosophy or Criticism with the same grace and ease with which one recounts a gallant adventure. Curiosity was the dominant passion of my Irishman, and he devoted himself more to the secret sciences <note>The "secret sciences" or "occult sciences" included practices like alchemy, astrology, and natural magic.</note> than to any other, although he was ignorant of none. My own inclination did not lead me there, but I had nevertheless acquired some smattering <note>Original: "teinture," literally a "dyeing" or "staining," used here to mean a superficial knowledge.</note> of them in Brandenburg, from a Jew who had rented me an apartment in his house, and whose integrity was so great that he entrusted me with the key to a private study, where he had a number of volumes dedicated to the usage and mysteries of the Cabala <note>The Cabala (Kabbalah) is an ancient tradition of Jewish mysticism involving the symbolic interpretation of scripture.</note>; Mr. Schits—that is my gentleman’s name—wanted to show me one

But in compensation, I unearthed one which gave rise to a long conversation. It bore the title **ON THE INTERMEDIATE STATE OF SOULS.** <note>Latin original: *De Medio Statu Animarum*. This likely refers to a work by Thomas White (1593–1676), an English Catholic priest whose controversial views on Purgatory and the state of the soul after death sparked significant debate.</note> I understood immediately that it might deal with Purgatory, and I was not mistaken. I asked Mr. Schits if mention was made therein of St. Patrick’s Purgatory <note>A famous site of pilgrimage on Station Island in Lough Derg, Ireland; legend claimed Christ revealed a cave there to St. Patrick that served as an entrance to Purgatory.</note>. He began to laugh and replied that it was not. I inquired of him what he thought of it, and whether this belief, which originated in his country, still endured there. He replied to me that it was current among the common people, but that people of quality <note>Original: "honnêtes gens." Refers to the educated, socially refined class who prized reason over superstition.</note> had always regarded it as a fable invented by monks, who had found the secret of fattening themselves on the illusions and credulity of fools. The abbey of these charlatans, he added, is not very far from one of my estates, and my own vassals <note>Tenants or subjects living on his land under the late feudal system.</note> carry their offerings there like all the rest. They are led with ceremony to the entrance of a deep and dark grotto, and while they say the prescribed prayers on their knees and trembling, the Monks slip into the depths of the grotto through underground passages, and there they cause horrible groans and claps of thunder to be heard, capable of terrifying the most resolute. In this period, the term referred to social interaction and conversation rather than trade.</note> he has with the rest of the world means that the place of his birth is unknown, and even more so his Religion. What is true is that he has a high concept of the Divinity <note>A term often used in the 17th and 18th centuries to refer to God in a way that bridged different theological or philosophical views.</note>, that he never speaks of it except with the deepest respect, and that he attributes virtues and effects to the names he gives it that surpass the imagination. They assert, as a truth, that he has spent two hundred years in this Hermitage <note>A secluded dwelling for a religious recluse or "hermit."</note> without being seen to eat or age; that he is equally informed of the future and the past; and that, finally, he has as much ease in communicating with the inhabitants of the other world <note>Referring to spirits, genies, or celestial beings.</note> as he shows reluctance to converse with the guests of this one. He has insights into everything, but his forte is the knowledge of the **secret sciences**, which he possesses in perfection, and which perhaps he acquired during his travels; for certainly he has made several, and I know from the man himself that his solitude has had long intervals. If you can apply yourself for a few hours to commit to memory a sketch of the principles of this **occult art**, and a portion of the terms specific to it, I am persuaded that they will serve you as a passport to be admitted to the participation of his mysteries.

If that is all that is required, Monsieur, I replied, I can depart this very hour; I am perfectly instructed in the jargon of the **Kabbalists** <note>Followers of Kabbalah, a form of Jewish mysticism. In the 17th and 18th centuries, Christian "Cabalism" also became a popular interest among European intellectuals.</note>, and my **Jew from Berlin** who had a rabbinical taste <note>Likely referring to a scholar of Jewish law and tradition from Berlin, a city which was a major center for Jewish intellectual life.</note> explained its principles to me so well that I myself can pose doubts <note>Original: *doutes*. "That is more than enough," replied **Mr. Schits**, "you may depart whenever you please; remember at least to be docile and to appear confident; forget your cavalier airs <note>The "airs Cavaliers" refers to the haughty, arrogant, or overly bold manners associated with the high-ranking military class of the time.</note>; modesty and simplicity will serve you well. I am going to order the preparations for your journey; you will be provided with good provisions, without examining whether they are necessary for you; for to tell you the truth, your grief has so shaped you to the **abstinence** <note>Refusal of food or physical comforts, here linked to the narrator's mourning and his studies.</note> of the **Philosophers**, that we do not yet know in my house if you know how to eat."

I thanked **Mr. Schits** for his kind attentions, and thought only of my departure. The servant he gave me as a guide was frightened by my design, and to make me abandon it, he said in secret to my valet de chambre, that his master was commanding him to lead me to the **Devil**, that this man we were going to see was the director of the **Sabbath** <note>Original: *sabat*. In the folklore of the era, the "Witches' Sabbath" was a midnight meeting of those practicing sorcery and worshipping the devil.</note>, and that he had far more communication with **Hell** <note>Original: *enfer*.</note> than with the earth; he added to this account a thousand prodigious circumstances capable of astonishing a weak and credulous mind: I

   On my way, I turned a thousand different thoughts over in my mind, and my heart was divided between fear and hope; at times I dreaded that the whimsy and eccentricity, which are almost always inseparable from hermits <note>Original: *solitaires*. In this context, it refers to those who live in isolation to pursue spiritual or alchemical secrets.</note>, would make my journey useless; at other times I listened agreeably to a certain inner premonition that promised me a pleasant and favorable welcome; and finally, this premonition prevailing over my distrust, I occupied myself seriously with all the things I was to propose to our <term>Philosopher</term> <note>A "Philosopher" in this era's literature typically refers to an alchemist or an initiate into the occult mysteries, seeking the "Philosopher's Stone."</note>. The companions of my journey, seeing me pensive, sought to amuse me.
   My valet, who is naturally cheerful and bold, said to me then: "It seems, Sir, that you are afraid of the **Devil**, but rest assured on my word; I carry the antidote to all **devilries** <note>Original: *diableries*. A term for acts of sorcery or the mischief caused by demons.</note> in your suitcase, and I have had the precaution to lock within it a bag of drugs fit to put to flight all the **infernal troops**; we have **St. John's Wort** <note>Original: *Millepertuis*. Historically nicknamed *fuga daemonum* (devil-chaser), this plant was widely believed to protect against spells and evil spirits.</note>, **Anise**, **Olive ashes**,

olives, **Rue** <note>Original: *Ruë*. A bitter herb, *Ruta graveolens*, historically used in folk medicine and as a protection against witchcraft.</note>, **Incense**, **Sulfur**, **white Hellebore** <note>Original: *Ellelbore blanc*. A toxic plant used in ancient and early modern medicine to treat madness by inducing purging.</note>, and above all that, a vial of **Essence** <note>In alchemy, an "essence" or "quintessence" refers to the purest, most concentrated form of a substance.</note>, before which **Lucifer** and the entire body of his colleagues would not stand for a single moment.

   This witty remark <note>Original: *saillie*. A sudden flash of wit or a clever retort.</note> cheered me greatly: I continued my walk, and the Heavens, which perhaps did not approve of it, flooded me with such a heavy rain that I was soaked to the bone. We had to endure this deluge, for there was neither tree nor house on our path to provide us shelter.

   When the Sun began to leave our horizon, and night was about to mark the end of the day, my guide discovered a small cottage where his Master had once taken refuge; we headed diligently in that direction, and having arrived, we were received with such a wild manner that I thought I was in a **Cave of Cyclopes** <note>A reference to the one-eyed giants of Greek mythology who lived in crude isolation.</note>. The Host, the Hostess, and their entire family possessed nothing of the qualities of man but his shape, and even that was so disguised by the strangeness of their clothes that the sight of **satyrs** <note>In mythology, satyrs are half-man, half-goat spirits of the wild, often used here to describe the "uncivilized" appearance of the peasants.</note>, as they are

I judged from that moment that it was best not to reason with them, nor to expect any humanity <note>In this context, "humanity" refers to the refined manners or kindness expected of "civilized" society.</note> from them. My valet set about making a fire to dry us. He was forced to extinguish it, because the thick and stinking smoke coming from the **earth that they burned** <note>This likely refers to peat or turf, partially decomposed vegetable matter used as fuel in regions where wood was scarce.</note> instead of wood would have driven us from this miserable dwelling. 

Luckily, we had provided ourselves with some candles which served to light us while we made the **first attack on our provisions** <note>A playful, military metaphor for eating hungrily.</note>; everything was cold, but apart from that, I never ate with such an appetite in my life. After our meal, a bed was prepared for us on the floor of the room, a layer of **untanned cowhides** <note>Original: *peaux de Vaches sans aprêt*. These are raw hides that have not been softened or treated, making them very stiff and uncomfortable.</note>; they were so dry and rough that **Morpheus** <note>The Greek god of dreams; the narrator suggests that even the god of sleep could not rest on such a bed.</note> himself, despite my weariness, would have spent the night without sleeping. 

I found my body all bruised, and my cloak, which, moreover, was soaked with water, could not protect me from a **general state of discomfort** <note>Original: *confusion générale*. This implies a total physical disorder or a feeling of being thoroughly "messed up" by the cold and the hard bed.</note>. My Guide and my Valet, for whom the hard floor served as a mattress, took their revenge on their insomnia

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We decamped from this infernal house at the **second cockcrow** <note>The second crowing of the rooster, traditionally marking the time shortly before dawn.</note>, after having paid handsomely for such a poor lodging. We returned to our path, and our journey was so successful that at **three hours of sun** <note>Meaning three hours after sunrise.</note> we found ourselves in sight of the Hermitage I was seeking. Its situation appeared to me very advantageous and even very pleasant, in comparison to the sterile and desert country one must cross—

These are three different terms that convey only a single idea: namely, a gold without mixture, without alloy <note>Original: "alliage." But the Genii <term>Génies: In this esoteric context, these are tutelary spirits or "guiding geniuses" that oversee the physical and social world, distinct from the angels concerned with the salvation of the soul.</term>, who are also Angels, and happy Angels, make themselves our assiduous and vigilant Directors in the natural order and in Politics <note>In the 18th-century context, "Politics" (la Politique) refers broadly to the management of human affairs, civil conduct, and social interactions.</note>. Every man has his own, in whatever state he may be, and the vigilance of these faithful Guides generally extends to everything related to our life, our health, our fortune, and our honor. If people are inclined to love us, if they take our side from the first sight, if we are favored in a Lawsuit, if unexpected generosities are shown to us, if we are applauded in conversations, if we foresee the ambushes laid for us and the dangers that threaten us, if we succeed in war, in Trade <term>Négoce: Specifically merchant commerce and business dealings.</term>, in the Arts; it is to the Genii that we are almost always indebted for these advantages, and it is they who procure these blessings for us; I say even more: it is only through them that one must seek the key to that *certain something* <note>original: "je ne fai quoi" — an indefinable quality, charm, or grace that makes a person or thing attractive without a clear explanation.</note> which

# THE GENII

two illustrious Romans who were well persuaded that their Republic owed its glory and its prosperity to the guidance of the **Genius** who took care of its fortune.

"That is well," I replied; "but why do we see so many people hated and unhappy, who do not find a single stone in their path that is not for them a stumbling block and a source of scandal <note>original: "pierre d'achopement & de scandale." The **Genii** do something similar: when the strength of their **ministry** <term>Ministére: The specific task or "office" assigned to a spirit to guide and protect a human being.</term> binds them to a beautiful soul or a pliant nature, they work with tireless application to attract all kinds of blessings to it; but nothing discourages them so much as being confined with souls that are hard, coarse, wild—

These gentlemen accepted the proposal and went together to the **refreshment room** <note>original: "buvette". Almost all of them had traveled after their studies; some knew **English** and **German**, others **Italian** and **Spanish**.

### THE SPIRITS

They understood nothing of what was shown to them, however. Then one of the group said to the others, "We find ourselves quite embarrassed, Gentlemen; Mr. de Somaize <note>Antoine Baudeau de Somaize (c. 1630–?), a French author and scholar famous for his 'Grand Dictionnaire des Prétieuses.' He was considered an expert on the linguistic trends and obscure vocabulary of his time.</note> is only a few steps from here. He is well-versed in the most unknown languages; let us send for him on the pretext of communicating an important matter. He will come at our request, for he is honorable and polite." This course of action pleased the assembly: Mr. de Somaize was brought in, and after some playful introductions, the piece of paper was placed before his eyes, asking him if he recognized the idiom <note>The specific language or dialect.</note> of the words being presented to him. 

"Yes," he replied, "it is Syriac <note>original: "Syriaque." An Eastern Aramaic language used by ancient Christian communities.</note> written in French <note>meaning the sounds of the Syriac language were transcribed using French phonetic spelling.</note>." 

"But," they asked him, "what do they mean?" 

"To provide an accurate translation," he replied, "it must be read thus: ***get out of your house, for it will fall into ruin today at nine o'clock in the evening***." 

The group burst into laughter at this translation <note>original: "version"</note>, and they claimed that the dream <note>original: "songe"</note> was nothing but a game and a story made up for amusement. One of the most prudent members of the company—who saw by the look of the concerned Counselor <note>referring to the magistrate/judge who had the dream</note> that he was not deceiving them—said to those laughing gentlemen

### THE ASSISTANTS ###

"I recognize from this gibberish," he replied, "the Author from whom you have taken this chimerical reasoning; you could have pushed the mockery further, and said, along with him, that this occurs because the soul contemplates itself circularly through the refraction of divine rays which illuminate its nature, and give its eyes the strength to cast its piercing gaze even into the thickest night of the future. As for me, who am less stiff-necked <note>The French "guindé" suggests someone overly formal, pretentious, or intellectually high-flown.</note> than that contemplative fellow, I distinguish, as did the ancients, the *songe* from the *rêve* <term>songe vs. rêve: In the 17th century, a "songe" was often considered a significant, coherent, or even prophetic vision, whereas a "rêve" was seen as a meaningless, chaotic product of the imagination.</term>. They must be attributed to two entirely different principles; for just as the *rêve* is an effect of the irregular movement of the animal spirits <term>animal spirits: In early modern science, these were thought to be extremely subtle fluids that flowed through the nerves and brain to facilitate thought and movement.</term>, which, traveling hither and thither within the cavities of the brain and striking traces distant from one another, excite in us ideas that have no connection, and which in their assembly represent to us things that are completely extravagant; the *songe*, on the contrary, is a perceptible inspiration from the Genius who leads us toward some good, or who tends to preserve us from some evil. It is upon this foundation that I could argue with some plausibility..."

### THE GENII ###

...that these people owe, to the foresight and counsels of their Genii <term>Genii: In this context, "Genii" (the plural of Genius) refers to personal protective spirits or "daemons" believed by some Neoplatonists to guide individual human lives.</term>, all the wise resolutions they have formed, and all the just measures they have taken, to extract themselves from trouble as successfully as they have done.

But without dwelling on mere plausibility <note>original: "vrai-ſemblance" — meaning the appearance of truth or a likely explanation.</note>, I pass, my Son, to certainty; and I am willing to tell you in confidence that it is easy for me to procure a secret interview with whichever Genius I see fit.

"What! you have the power," I said to him, "to converse with the geniuses of others whenever you please?"

"Without a doubt," he replied, "it is a privilege that Philosophers have preserved for themselves with great care, and which they regard, so to speak, as the most beautiful flower in their garden. They enjoy it quietly and in secret. It would be dangerous to make it known; for the Common People <note>original: "le Peuple" — here referring to the uneducated masses who were often viewed by the elite as superstitious.</note>, who are without reason and who put the Devil everywhere they cannot understand, view these glorious communications as operations of magic; and the People of the Court, who hardly venture further beyond the senses than..."

->A S S I S T A N T S. 83<-

...the People, they would treat them as madness and illusion, as witnessed by what happened to a Marquis who commanded in Brittany <note>original: "Bretagne"</note>.

Some time ago, there were seen in that country two great armies, which drew themselves up for battle in a plain between three villages, at the hour when the peasant returns from work to give himself over to rest. As they were on the point of charging one another, a third army arrived and took them both on the flank; with a terrifying noise of musketry and cannon, it put them to flight, dispersed them, and disappeared along with them. A vast number of peasants witnessed this, and some even approached these troops so closely that they could describe their faces and their clothing. The news of this apparition <term>apparition: A supernatural appearance or ghost-like vision, often of a military nature in 17th-century folklore.</term> soon spread throughout the province; nothing else was spoken of in Nantes and Rennes, which are near the place where this scene was enacted. The Marquis-Commandant (I withhold his name because of the injustices done to him) was informed of it like the others; he had a kind of official report <term>procès-verbal: A formal written record of an event or statement, often used in legal or administrative proceedings.</term> drawn up, and based on this well-detailed report, he made a

If it is true that these **Genies** <note>The speaker is referring to guardian spirits or intermediaries, a common theme in 17th-century spiritual philosophy.</note> do not perish, I interrupted, you must agree, my **Father**, that when war, plague, and famine, and all the other scourges of mankind provide an abundant harvest for the **Fates** <note>original: "la parque"; in Roman mythology, the Fates (Parcae) were the three goddesses who presided over the birth and life of humans, often associated here with death.</note>, many of these **Genies** must be discharged <note>original: "réformez"; here used in a military sense, meaning to be retired or let go from active duty when a unit is downsized.</note> and find themselves forced to retreat home to live in idleness, unless they are placed on half-year leave <note>original: "semestres"; referring to a system of rotating service.</note> or made to serve in shifts <note>original: "par quartier"; a system where officials or soldiers served for a three-month "quarter" of the year.</note>.

Do you consider them quite unfortunate, he said, to be relieved of a tiring job, for which only the command of **Heaven** and a certain weight that inclines them toward us can make such cares agreeable? But they do not remain without occupation; for besides the fact that they willingly associate with one another to watch over the steps of the same person, their activity turns entirely toward **God**, who is their unique center and the essential object of their **contemplation**.

<summary>In this dialogue, the characters discuss the immortality of guardian spirits (Genies) and humorously ponder what happens to these spirits when mass death reduces the human population, concluding that their primary purpose is the contemplation of God.</summary>

### THE GENIES

Revere, my Son, these immortal and blessed spirits, without paying heed to the lowliness of their duties; they are base and humiliating if one compares them with those of our **Guardian Angels**; but consider that all the glory and all the grandeur of the **Ministers of the Most High** <note>original: "Ministres du très Haut"; a theological term for the various ranks of angels and spirits serving God’s will.</note> is to obey Him and to be faithful to Him. He is the Author of **nature**, just as He is the Author of **grace** and of the societies that are formed on the earth, as well as those He assembles in **Heaven**. Since the benefits of each are different, the **Angels** and the **Genies** also work differently to acquire them for us. The **People** <note>The "People" refers to the uneducated masses or common folk, often contrasted with "philosophers" in 17th-century texts.</note> do not enter into these mysteries; they prefer to attribute the temporal and extraordinary successes that happen to them to the **Devil** and to **fortune**, rather than to the vigilance of the **Genies**.

   How fortunate you are, I said to him, to be sheltered from these popular illusions; I abjure them from the bottom of my heart; your principles are sensible, and I am persuaded that if **Sponde** <note>Henri de Sponde (1568–1643) was a French jurist and later the Bishop of Pamiers, known for his continuation of Baronius's Ecclesiastical Annals.</note> had known them, he would have shown mercy to the **Predictions of Nostradamus** <note>Michel de Nostredame (1503–1566), the famous French physician and reputed seer whose quatrains remain famous for their cryptic prophecies.</note> which he treats as trifles and nonsense; and instead of

"You are taking the right path," said **Magnamara**, "stay with it, and never stray from the principles I have revealed to you, which are so solid."

"I would bind myself by oath to follow them," I said to him, "if you would have the kindness to excuse me from the rigor of a **Novitiate** <term>Novitiate: A probationary period of trial and training required before a person is fully admitted into a secret society or religious order.</term>, which is going to deprive me for too long of communication with my **Genie**."

As soon as I had finished speaking, he began to contradict with great fire everything I had put forward regarding the excellence of our nature.

This was not always the case; for according to the most enlightened soul of our Century <note>original: "Siécle" — likely a reference to René Descartes or Nicolas Malebranche, philosophers who famously compared the body to a machine or a clock.</note>, it <note>The French "elle" refers to the soul (l'âme).</note> had, before the sin of the first man <note>the biblical Fall of Adam, which in theology supposedly disrupted the soul's perfect control over the body.</note>, the power to unhook the pendulum and the weights of the Clock, to detach the bell ropes, to take the bell down itself, and to carry it, so to speak, out of the belfry, for fear that the harmony of the carillon might make it feel desires and pleasures that it could not experience without wounding its innocence and its liberty.

Poor Sovereign! Feeble Governess! There is no sailor so puny that he does not know the number of ropes on his Ship and the uses for which they are intended; the soul is not so clever—it is ignorant of the structure of the internal parts of its body; their operations are imperceptible to it, and it never notices them. And when it is instructed in them, it is always on the report of others.

# LE GNOME

<note>The French word "âme" (soul) is feminine; therefore, the text uses the pronoun "elle" (she) throughout to refer to the soul.</note> Her sensations are the source of her pleasures and her pains; yet is there anything more unknown to her than their <term>economy<note>The internal organization or functional system of the body.</note></term>? She does not know if she moves toward sensory objects, or if they come to her, or rather if she does not perceive them in Him who created them <note>A likely reference to the "Vision in God" theory by philosopher Nicolas Malebranche, who argued we perceive ideas through God rather than directly from objects.</note>. She strips herself of her feelings to clothe beings in them to whom they do not belong. She regards colors, tastes, smells, sounds, and light—and a thousand other things—as foreign, though they spring from the depths of her nature and are her own modes of being. It is through the senses that she communicates with all the bodies that surround her own. What is required to break this communication? A thin skin takes away her sight; a fine film of dirt deprives her of taste and hearing; a drop of <term>humor<note>original: "humeur" — in early medicine, these were the fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) believed to determine health and temperament.</note></term> that obstructs a nerve, or an atom of dust slipping into the <term>ureter<note>The duct by which urine passes from the kidney to the bladder.</note></term>, blocks the path of her messengers and robs her of the knowledge of what is happening in her body. In short, it takes almost nothing to throw all her faculties into disorder and inaction.

If she <note>The author refers to the soul as "she," following the feminine gender of the French word "l'âme."</note> is so excellent, and if she possesses the power and strength that her <term>panegyrists<note>People who deliver speeches or writings of high praise.</note></term> attribute to her, why does she not pierce this skin? She animates the entire machine <note>The "machine" refers to the human body, a common philosophical metaphor in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note>; she is everywhere, and yet if some storm or tempest arises within her domains <note>Meaning the various parts and systems of the living body.</note>, instead of calming them herself, she always has recourse to some other distant soul. This other soul, being less united to the body than she is, is also less informed of its needs and ought to take less interest in them. 

What would the King of Siam think if the Emperor <note>A reference to the Holy Roman Emperor, whose seat of power was in Vienna.</note> addressed him to be informed of the disorders occurring in Vienna? Would he not take this Prince for a man who had lost his senses? And if propriety obliged him to respond to the request, what could he say but these words: "How do you expect me to know what is happening where I am not, if you are ignorant of what is occurring where you are?" Your soul is even more foolish and more worthy of pity than that German Majesty would be.

...one does him too much honor <note>original: "fait trop de grace." Man is, in their regard, a stripped King, a King in a painting <note>original: "un Roi en peinture." This refers to a "paper tiger" or a titular ruler who has the appearance of authority—like a portrait—but possesses no actual power over the subjects he claims to rule.</note>, who takes pride in a title without authority. What persuades me, and should convince you, that nothing is taken from him when animals are placed on equal footing with him, is that the Almighty, who is the absolute Master of ranks and fortunes, advises you to take the model of your own conduct from the behavior of animals. He refers the scatterbrain to the Serpent, the deceiver to the Dove, the lazy to the Ant, and the one who distrusts Providence to the Birds of Heaven <note>These references draw on biblical moral lessons: the wisdom of the serpent and innocence of the dove (Matthew 10:16), the diligence of the ant (Proverbs 6:6), and the trust of the birds (Matthew 6:26).</note>, who, without worrying about sowing or harvesting, nevertheless live and subsist throughout the year.

I add to this two remarks, which you can make every day, and which should strike you until they convince you of the truth of what I have told you so far. Their ability to conceive ideas is entirely blocked, their reason has suffered shipwreck, and they do and say nothing but extravagances. Consider that the **fire of the blood** <term>fire of the blood: In early modern medicine, "innate heat" or "vital fire" in the blood was believed to be the engine of both life and thought.</term> acts upon the soul as light acts upon the eyes, and that their impressions are entirely similar. With too little light, one sees objects only confusedly; with enough light, one sees them very distinctly; and with too much light, one is dazzled, and cannot see a thing. With too little fire in the blood, one has views and knowledge that are confused and limited; with a **regulated fire**, one has just, fine, and very clear views. With an **excessive fire**, one has only strange and lost views. And why? ## 162

...[we] have abstained from wagering with you, because, given the way you are fashioned today, and because one can deal with you according to your own principles, there is an equal risk of loss and gain—which is less desirable than our state. For nothingness <note>original: "le néant"; here referring to the total cessation of existence. The Gnome argues that total annihilation at death is a "safety" against the possibility of eternal hell.</note> saves us from eternal misery, and at the same time removes the sensation of all those losses you recently exaggerated. Live then in peace, and let us die in the same manner; we do not envy your happiness. Benefit from your past misfortunes, and follow to the end the path to immortality that has been reopened to you, without further troubling those to whom your corruption has closed it forever.

I guarantee you, I said, your tranquility for the future; it is advantageous to have no relationship with you. [...truth] looks at herself constantly from being tarnished by your slanders. "Potency" (or power) is the potential to exist or act, while "act" (or actuality) is the realization of that potential. Aristotle's definition of the soul as the "first act of a physical body" was notoriously difficult for his contemporaries to grasp.</term>.

This famous Decree, pronounced in the tone of a Master and a Sovereign, was received differently by this venerable company. The *Courtier-Philosophers* made faces <note>original: "mines"; expressions or gestures</note> to show their admiration. The mockers, alluding to the terms of the Decree, said that the soul was too fortunate to have fallen into the hands of *Aristotle*, and that if he did not give it much light, in return he granted it much power and authority <note>The mockers are punning on Aristotle's use of "puissance," which means both "potency" (a philosophical term) and "power" or "authority" in a political sense.</note>. The Satirists, whose imaginary power <note>original: "puissance fantastique"; another pun on Aristotle's terminology</note> did not increase their fortunes, called him a Charlatan, and whispered quietly that he had called them there to sell them his balm. The independent thinkers, who made up the largest number, kept a dismal silence,

It is upon this foundation that they imposed upon their followers the law of penetrating the meaning of the **Enigma** he had cast out at random, which engaged them in such painful vigils and research that it cost nearly all of them their reason and their health.

It is a pity, I said to **Magnamara**, that the **Decree of this Assembly** has not come down to us. The malcontents surely must have carried off some collated copy. It remained for a long time in the Archives of the **Areopagus** <term>Areopagus: The "Hill of Ares" in Athens, which functioned as the city's high court and supreme council.</term>, he continued; but since then, it has been swept up in the revolutions and ruins of that flourishing **Republic**.

The fruit we must draw from the faint memory that tradition [has preserved]...

It is necessary for us to know that God created our soul, and that He made it for Himself; but it matters little to us to know whether it is a stranger in the body or if it is native to it; whether it arrives there after it is formed, or if it helps to form it. I believe them all to be equal, he replied to me, since they all have the same extraction <term>extraction: spiritual lineage or origin</term>; since they all come forth immediately from the hand of the Most High; since they all equally bear the features of His likeness and His Image, which is the mark of their greatness and their nobility; and finally, since they all have the same beginning, the same end, and the same duties. distinguishes the soul of man from that
of other animals.

<summary>The author argues that human souls lose their inherent divine nobility when they prioritize worldly matters over spiritual duties, effectively becoming like animals. He also rejects contemporary philosophical views that describe animal souls as either non-existent machines or purely material entities.</summary>

### LE GNOME

...their soul is spiritual and endowed with rea-

which nevertheless make its inner workings
and duties entirely different; for be-

Him—that is to say, not destined to possess Him—

consequently it must end along with the
goal it proposes for itself, and toward which
all its views and all its movements
tend; death certainly respecting
only God and His Image.

It seems to me, I said to Magnamara,
that the Gnome Prince <note>In the context of this 18th-century mystical dialogue, the Gnome Prince represents the leader of the elemental spirits of the earth.</note> agrees well enough
with you on this principle of the immor-
tality of our souls. It is not
this agreement that constitutes his error,
he replied; he only goes astray in
asserting that the Image of God was ob-

man <note>Original: "le crime du premier homme" — a reference to the Fall of Adam and the concept of Original Sin.</note>, and that we have lost,
through this obscuring, the privilege of
immortalizing ourselves, and others through
our mediation; which is not the case. This
Image was only obscured and, in
some manner, erased; but the foundation,

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 172 regarding the nature of the soul and the "Image of God." It completes the sentence: "...but the foundation, as you have wisely said, to this stubborn Gnome..."</meta>

### IRRECONCILABLE.

**stubborn Gnome**, has remained with us with all the prerogatives that are attached to it. This is the sentiment of the greatest and most profound **Doctor of the Church** <note>Likely referring to St. Augustine of Hippo, whose theology centered on the "Imago Dei" (Image of God) and the soul's dependence on God's presence.</note>. He maintains in a thousand places in his books that this **sacred Image** is so proper and essential to our soul that if it were separated from it for a single moment, it would immediately fall back into the **nothingness** from which it emerged. And to make us understand the mystery of this **sacred Image**, and the manner in which it is formed and imprinted within us, he compares our soul to a mirror’s glass. The **Divine Word** <term>Original: "Verbe divin," referring to the Logos or the second person of the Trinity</term>, he says, looks at itself in this **mystical mirror**, and produces its Image there; and upon this continual gaze, which becomes reciprocal between them, depends the entire being and the entire duration of this Image and of the foundation upon which it is imprinted; so that this Image would vanish entirely and cease to be if it ceased for one moment to be looked upon; just as we see the image of a man looking at himself in a mirror disappear as soon as he moves away and no longer presents himself before it.

<meta>catchword: Nor</meta>

<summary>The author uses a mirror analogy, attributed to a Doctor of the Church, to explain that the human soul’s existence depends entirely on God's continuous gaze. Without this divine reflection, the "Image of God" within man would vanish into nothingness.</summary>
<keywords>Image of God, Imago Dei, St. Augustine, Divine Word, Logos, mystical mirror, ontological dependence, nothingness, immortality</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the soul's immortality. The previous page used the analogy of a mirror to show how the human soul exists only through God’s constant gaze. Here, the speaker contrasts this divine privilege with the "soul of beasts."</meta>

->### LE GNOME<-

Do not be surprised, then, if the soul of beasts, as spiritual as I make it, perishes with their body. Everything that has begun must end according to the natural order; **nothingness** <note>The concept of "le néant" or the void from which creation was drawn.</note> claims what it gave. Our soul would follow this same fate, were it not privileged by virtue of the **august characters** <term>Original: "caractéres," referring to the indelible spiritual marks or the "Image of God" imprinted on the soul</term> that the reason of the Most High has engraved in the depths of its Being: it participates in the Rights of the sovereign **Truth**, which is always present to it, and honors are rendered to it just as one does to the portraits of Sovereigns <note>Just as a king's portrait is treated with respect because of whom it represents, the human soul is honored because it is an image of God.</note>. You will tell me, perhaps, that the goodness of the Lord would be wounded if His **Wisdom** allowed spirits He has enlightened with His radiance to perish without pity; and I answer you that it would suffer no injury even if all creatures were annihilated; because appearing or disappearing, creating or ceasing to create, are equally just in God, and there would be no more reason to complain of the premature end of the world than of the delay of its production.

"Would I be permitted, my Father," I said to him, "to ask you the reason for—"

<meta>catchword: the-</meta>

<summary>The speaker explains that while animal souls are spiritual, they are mortal by nature, unlike human souls which God has "privileged" with an eternal character. He argues that God’s justice remains perfect regardless of whether He chooses to sustain or annihilate His creations.</summary>

<keywords>animal souls, natural order, immortality, sovereign Truth, divine justice, creation, annihilation, image of God</keywords>

# IRRECONCILABLE.

[the reason for] which the Most High, whose designs and wills are always most equitable, abstained for so long from creating worshippers for Himself. I am going to teach it to you," he replied. He began to speak on this subject; but suddenly the words died in his mouth; he became pensive. His eyes seemed fixed upon the earth, or rather, upon the pavement of his cell, and he took on the look and manner of a man who was extraordinarily focused. I believed he was thinking seriously about what he should answer me, but it was nothing of the sort. He said to me as if waking from a deep sleep: "I ask your pardon, my Son, if I am obliged to leave you; here is my **Genius** <term>Original: "Génie," referring to a tutelary spirit or guiding daemon often found in Platonic and occult traditions.</term> who warns me that an Envoy from the Sages of China has arrived in my study, to propose on their behalf some important difficulties, which the **Book of Enoch** <note>An ancient Jewish religious work, traditionally attributed to the great-grandfather of Noah, which describes the fall of the Watchers and various celestial secrets.</note> and the **Collection of Precepts left to the Children of Noah** <note>Refers to the "Seven Laws of Noah," a set of universal moral laws found in Jewish tradition said to apply to all of humanity.</note> have given rise to in those lands. They await my decisions. This messenger is a **Sylph** <term>A mythological elemental being of the air.</term> who was sent this morning from **Tonkin** <note>A historical region in northern Vietnam, then known as Tonquin.</note>, and who must return there before midnight. I must attend to him; calm and solitude are ne-

<meta>This page concludes the narrative encounter. The narrator has been feigning interest in the occult and theological mysteries presented by a recluse who claimed to be visited by a supernatural messenger from China.</meta>

# THE GNOME

176

...cessary for that. <note>Completing the word "necessary" from the previous page's catchword.</note> I civilly stated to him that I would be in despair to cause him the slightest distraction, and after several embraces and many thanks, I made him a very humble bow and separated from him, with the promise to see him again soon and to return eagerly to draw new instructions from the source of his insights. I was very glad to have this pretext to distance myself from a house where doors were useless; for in the end, this **Express** <term>A fast messenger or courier.</term>, whom I suspected of being the **Courier of Merlin** <note>The narrator is being satirical here, comparing the supposedly supernatural messenger to the legendary wizard Merlin to imply the whole affair is a fantasy or a fraud.</note>, could only have entered there through the chimney. I concealed my thoughts and departed to communicate them to **Mr. Schits** <note>Likely a phonetic spelling of Schütz, a common name among German officials or military officers of the period.</note>, with whom I amused myself greatly over the role I had just played. I repeated it to him so exactly that nothing was missing. He laughed with all his heart to hear me acting the **Philosopher** and the **Theologian**, I whose entire science seemed to be limited to forming a **Battalion** and giving it all its faces <note>A military reference to the narrator's actual profession; "giving faces" refers to maneuvering a square of infantry to face different directions of attack.</note>. This game continued for several days, after which a letter from the **Count of Schomberg** <note>The Schombergs were a famous international military family; this likely refers to a commander the narrator served under.</note> made me return to **Berlin**, to await there the predictions of my true **Genius** <term>A play on words: meaning both his guiding spirit and his own natural intellect/inclination.</term>.

->THE END.<-

<summary>The narrator ends his satirical performance as a student of the occult, revealing his true identity as a soldier before departing for Berlin on official orders. He mocks the recluse's belief in supernatural visitors, suggesting the "spirit" was merely someone coming down the chimney.</summary>
